STAFF REPORT 
 
To:   Snyderville Basin Planning Commission   
From:   Ray Milliner, County Planner  
Date of Meeting: October 22, 2024 
Type of Item:  Conditional Use Permit - Public Hearing, Possible Action 
Process:  Administrative Review 
File #:   24-056 
 
 
Recommendation: Staff has reviewed the application for compliance with all standards 
in the Snyderv ille Basin Development Code (Code) and found that it m eets the minimum 
required for approval.  Staff recommends that the Snyderville Basin Planning  Commission 
(SBPC) review the proposed Conditional Use Permit, and approve the  application, pursuant t o 
the findings of fact, conclusions of law, and conditions of approval found in this Staff Report. 
 
Project Description 
 
Project Name:   Maverik Convenience Store 
Applicant(s):   Maverik Inc. (James Copeland Representative) 
Property Owner(s):  Big Hoss Properties LLC. 
Location:   Old Hwy 40 and Atkinson Road 
Zone District: Service Commercial (SC), Hillside Stewardship (HS) Lower Silver 
Creek Soils Overlay zones 
Parcel Number and Size: Parcel #SS-65-A-3, 7.68 acres 
Type of Process:  Conditional Use Permit 
Final Land Use Authority: Planning Commission  
 
Proposal 
 
Maverick Inc. would like to build a gas station with an attached convenience store at the corner 
of Old HWY 40 and Atkinson Road (Quinn’s Junction). The proposed fuel station is to have a 
5,951 square foot convenience store with 16 vehicle fueling positions, an additional 16 future 
fueling positions and 6 commercial truck / RV fueling positions, for a total of 38 fueling 
positions. 
 
 
 
5
2 
 
Vicinity Map 
 
 
 
 
Background 
 
Maverick Inc. Has applied for a conditional use permit for a gasoline service station with 
convenience store at the corner of Old HWY 40 and Atkinson Road. Details of the project 
include: 
 
• The lot is approximately 7.68 acres. 
• There is a 2.96-acre wetland area on the northeast side of the lot. No construction is 
proposed in the wetland area.  
• The lot is in both the Service Commercial (SC) and Hillside Stewardship (HS) zones.  
• The lot is in the Lower Silver Creek Soils Overlay Zone.  
6
3 
 
• Proposed construction will take place within the area zoned SC. No construction 
proposed in the HS zone. 
• The proposed fuel station is to have a 5,951 square foot convenience store with 16 
vehicle fueling positions, an additional 16 future fueling positions and 6 commercial 
truck / RV fueling positions, for a total of 38 fueling positions. 
• There is a Fueling canopy proposed above all pumps. 
• Underground fuel tanks are proposed on the west side of property in front of the main 
pump area.  
• 30 parking stalls proposed along the southern side of the building (this number has been 
reduced from 37 stalls). 
• One ingress/egress access point proposed from Old HWY 40. 
• One ingress only access point proposed from Atkinson Road.  
• The applicant has secured water rights for the use which will be provided by Summit 
Water.  
• Sewer service for the use will be provided by the Snyderville Basin Water Reclamation 
District.  
 
In May of 2024, the County Council, with a positive recommendation from the Snyderville Basin 
Planning Commission, approved an amendment to the Snyderville Basin Development Code to 
make a “Gas Station With an Attached Convenience Store” a conditional use in the SC zone. 
This amendment enabled the applicant to apply for this CUP. 
 
August 13, and September 10, 2024, Planning Commission Meetings 
 
This item was heard by the Planning Commission on August 13, and September 10, 2024. The 
Commission requested more information on: 
 
Accessibility for bikers and hikers from the Rail Trail: Access to the property from the rail trail 
will be provided by an enhanced trail along HWY 248 then up New Old HWY 40 to the site and 
finally through a pathway around the perimeter of the of the parking area to a bike parking 
area. This route is the only reasonable access for bikes for the following reasons: 
 
o The County cannot legally require a property owner to obtain an easement from 
an adjacent property owner.  
o Direct access from their own property would cross the wetland/toxic area where 
development is prohibited by the environmental covenant on the site. 
o Staff contacted The Utah Division of State Parks who stated that they will not 
allow any new connections to the trail as it would potentially increase 
maintenance costs of the trail.  The County is currently negotiating a purchase 
of the Rail Trail from State Parks, but to date nothing has been confirmed.   
 
7
4 
 
Onsite parking. The applicant is requesting 31 onsite parking spaces. The maximum allowed by 
code is 21. Chapter 10-4-9 of the Development Code states that parking that exceeds the 
amount indicated may be permitted only after the applicant submits a parking study for 
comparable uses which demonstrates that a higher demand can be anticipated. The applicant 
submitted a parking study that was reviewed by the County Transportation Manager as well as 
the County Engineer. Both concurred that the increased number of spaces is warranted (letter 
from Transportation manager is attached to this staff report Exhibit A).    
 
The photo below shows the parking situation for the Chevron station located at Silver Summit 
(near Home Depot). This station has approximately 38 striped parking spaces with more parking 
in the gravel area behind the store. Staff asked the manager what all the parking in the gravel 
area was, he stated it is employee parking, and a few long-term spaces. At peak times, this 
location is under parked. 
  
 
Figure 1: Chevron Station at Silver Summit 
 
Electric Vehicle Charging Stations: The Commission requested that the applicant install Electric 
Vehicle Charging Stations. The applicant has agreed to install two Level II chargers in the 
parking spaces closest to the trash enclosure with an additional two spaces built as “ev ready” 
(with all the infrastructure in place), so that when demand increases, more chargers can be 
easily installed. A condition of approval has been added to this report.  
 
8
5

The subject property is in the SC zone district.  
 
Code Requirement Analysis Finding 
SC zone intent 
The purpose of the service commercial/light 
industrial (SC) zone district is to provide 
appropriate locations within the Snyderville Basin 
for service commercial and light industrial uses, 
recognizing that such uses provide vital support 
functions for the basin's resort and residential 
economy. 
In May of 2024, the County 
Council approved an amendment 
to the Development Code making 
Gas Stations with attached 
Convenience stores a conditional 
use in the SC zone.

COMPLIES 
 
Chapter 8, General Provisions  
The proposed gas station is subject to the following requirements 
 
A. Separation: No service station shall be located within two hundred feet (200') of a single-
family dwelling or within five hundred feet (500') of another service station. COMPLIES 
 
Analysis: There are no single-family homes within 200 feet of the lot and no other 
gas stations within 500 feet of the lot.  
 
B. Distance to Intersection, Road: No more than one service station may be located within 
two hundred feet (200’) of the intersection of any major road, arterial road or collector 
road with another major road, arterial road or collector road. COMPLIES 
 
Analysis: The lot is approximately 400 feet from the intersection of HWY 248 and 
HWY 40. Nonetheless, there are no other service stations in the immediate area.  
 
C. Screening: An opaque screening treatment shall be provided on all sides of the property 
that are located adjacent to residentially zoned and residentially used property. COMPLIES 
 
10
7 
 
Analysis: There are no residential developments on any side of the property. The 
north, east and west sides are industrial, and the south side is a toxic waste 
dump. Staff finds that there is no need for opaque screening.  
 
Conditional Use Permit Criteria for Approval 
No conditional use permit shall be approved unless the applicant demonstrates that: 
  
Standard 1: The use is in accordance with the General Plan. COMPLIES 
  
Analysis: The SC zone is in the Quinn’s Junction neighborhood area of the Snyderville 
Basin General Plan. The Neighborhood Description in that section States: 
 
“The types of uses in this area support a viable economy in Summit County. 
Recognizing this, the continued use of and the expansion of additional service 
commercial and industrial uses should be considered, especially near the existing 
development located in the northeast quadrant of SR-248 and US-40. 
Appropriate uses may include general offices, business parks, manufacturing, 
and other service related and industrial uses.”   
 
The proposed gas station is consistent with the General Plan statement that service-
related uses should be expanded within the zone.  
 
Standard 2: The use conforms to all applicable provisions of this Title, including, but not limited 
to, any applicable provisions of this Section and Chapter 4 of this Title, the General Plan, and 
State and Federal regulations;

Standard 3: The use is not detrimental to public health, safety and welfare;  
 
Analysis: The use does have the potential for environmental issues, mostly related to 
the storage and distribution of fuel. However, these impacts are regulated and 
mitigated through the review and oversight process in the International Building Code 
as well as Utah State requirements for underground tanks. The potential impacts of the 
toxic soil in the area have been mitigated through EPA approved remediation. The 
property owner has signed an agreement with the EPA and DEQ finalizing the process.  
 
There are no service stations on HWY 248 between Kamas and Park City. This means 
that any resident living along that corridor (including Brown’s Canyon) must drive to 
Kamas or Park City to get gas. Allowing a service station use in the Quinn’s Junction area 
could reduce vehicle trips into Park City, Kamas or Silver Summit by providing an 
alternative for those residents.   
 
Standard 4: The use is appropriately located with respect to public facilities  
   
Analysis: The use is appropriate for the site. All services necessary to support the use 
are available on site. The biggest issue is the impact of the use on Old HWY 40 and the 
increased demand for it. The applicant has worked with the Engineering and 
Transportation Departments to make improvements and relocate Old HWY 40 that will 
help both the applicant and drivers on 228 and the frontage road.    
 
Standard 5: The use is compatible with the existing neighborhood character and with the 
character and purpose provision of the applicable zoning district and will not adversely affect 
surrounding land uses.  
 
Analysis: This area of Summit County is generally industrial. There are no residential 
uses allowed in the immediate area. There are two major arterial highways intersecting 
nearby with loads of traffic. Conditions of approval have been included in this report 
designed to mitigate the impacts of the use on traffic in the area.    
 
Special CUP Standards: In addition to the standards established in this section and in chapter 4 
of [the Code] for uses, all conditional uses within a zoning district shall conform to the following 
standards and criteria:

COMPLIES 
 
Recommendation 
Staff recommends that the Snyderville Basin Planning Commission (SBPC) review the proposed 
Conditional Use Permit, and approve the application, pursuant to the following findings of fact, 
conclusions of law, and conditions of approval. 
 
Findings of Fact: 
 
1. Parcel #SS-65-A-3 is located at the intersection of Atkinson Road and Old HWY 40.  
2. The lot is approximately 7.68 acres. 
3. There is a 2.96-acre wetland area on the northeast side of the lot. No construction is 
proposed in the wetland area. 
4. There are slopes exceeding 30% between the wetland area and the proposed 
development area. No construction is proposed on the steep slope area.   
5. The wetland area is under an environmental covenant which prohibits construction or 
development in that area. 
 
6. The lot is in both the Service Commercial (SC) and Hillside Stewardship (HS) zones.  
7. The lot is in the Lower Silver Creek Soils Overlay Zone.  
8. In May of 2024, the County Council approved an amendment to the Development Code 
making Gas Stations with attached Convenience stores a conditional use in the SC zone. 
9. Proposed construction will take place within the area zoned SC. No construction 
proposed in the HS zone. 
15
12 
 
10. The proposed fuel station is to have a 5,951 square foot convenience store with 16 
vehicle fueling positions, an additional 16 future fueling positions and 6 commercial 
truck / RV fueling positions, for a total of 38 fueling positions. 
11. Fueling canopy proposed above all pumps. 
12. Underground fuel tanks proposed on west side of property in front of main pump area.  
13. The applicant is requesting 31 onsite parking spaces.  
14. The maximum number of parking spaces allowed by code is 21.  
15. Chapter 10-4-9 of the Development Code states that parking that exceeds the amount 
indicated may be permitted only after the applicant submits a parking study for 
comparable uses which demonstrates that a higher demand can be anticipated.  
16. The applicant submitted a parking study that was reviewed by the County 
Transportation Manager as well as the County Engineer. Both Concurred that the 
increased number of spaces is warranted  
17. One ingress/egress access point proposed from Old HWY 40. 
18. One ingress only access point proposed from Atkinson Road.  
19. In January of 2021, the applicant signed an agreement with the EPA and DEQ outlining 
the process that would allow them to develop the lot.  
20. The agreement requires the property owner to conduct a survey of the property with 
specific requirements that would need to be followed to test the lot and to receive 
approval to begin development.  
21. In January of 2024, the applicant conducted an environmental impact study of the lot.  
22. The study followed the requirements of both the County and the EPA.  
23. The study found that the northwest side of the lot is acceptable for commercial uses, 
but that the wetland area is managed through an environmental covenant and is 
therefore undevelopable.  
24. Because this is a new development and it is not under a development agreement or 
consent agreement, the project must meet the requirements of Chapter 5, Affordable 
Housing in the Snyderville Basin Development code. 
25. Based on the formula in Chapter 5, the applicant is responsible for .35 AUEs (affordable 
Unit Equivalents).  
26. The applicant has chosen to pay an in-lieu fee of $30,313.50 instead of building an 
affordable unit.  
27. The maximum allowed building height in the SC zone is 32 feet above existing grade or 
finished grade, whichever results in the smaller building.  
28. The proposed structure will be 29 feet above finished grade.  
29. The SC zone requires a 80 foot front yard setback, and 12 foot side and rear yard 
setbacks.  
30. The proposed setbacks are 80 feet in the front, 67 feet and 38 feet on the sides and 57 
feet in the rear.  
31. The proposed use has 30 parking spaces. 
32. All necessary utilities are available on site.  
33. The applicant has secured water rights for the use which will be provided by Summit 
Water.  
16
13 
 
34. Sewer service for the use will be provided by the Snyderville Basin Water Reclamation 
District.  
35. All proposed loading and unloading will take place on site.  
36. The perimeter landscape area is 18,900 square feet or approximately 28% of the parking 
area.  
37. There are no service stations on HWY 248 between Kamas and Park City.  
38. This area of Summit County is industrial.  
39. There are no residential uses allowed in the immediate area.  
40. There are two major arterial highways intersecting nearby. 
41. All proposed loading and unloading of merchandise, including fuel will occur on site.  
 
Conclusions of Law:  
 
1. The use is in accordance with the general plan. 
2. The use conforms to all applicable provisions of the Code, including, but not limited to, 
any applicable provisions of Section 10-3-5, Chapter 4, and state and federal regulations. 
3. The use is not detrimental to public health, safety, and welfare. 
4. The use is appropriately located with respect to public facilities. 
5. The use is compatible with the existing neighborhood character and with the purpose 
provisions of the applicable zone district and will not affect surrounding land uses. 
 
Conditions of Approval: 
 
1. Prior to the issue of a certificate of occupancy, the applicant shall install a southbound 
left turn pocket at the Maverik access along Old Hwy 40.  
2. Prior to the issue of a certificate of occupancy the applicant shall install a northbound 
deceleration lane at the Maverik access along Old Hwy 40. 
3. Maverik will be required to work with UDOT and install a traffic signal at the SR-248/Old 
Hwy 40 intersection. 
4. A certificate of occupancy shall not be issued until the County Engineer and 
Transportation Director are satisfied that the road improvements have been made.  
5. The applicant shall pay an in-lieu fee of $30,313.50 to meet the affordable housing 
requirement of the Development Code.  
6. Two electric vehicle charging stations shall be installed in the two parking spaces 
nearest the trash enclosure.  
7. Two additional parking spaces shall be Electric Vehicle Ready. Electric Vehicle Ready 
shall be defined as: spaces that are designed and constructed to include a fully wired 
circuit for an Electric Vehicle Charging Station, including conduit and wiring with the 
electrical service capacity necessary to serve the power outlets to allow for the future 
installation of Level 2 Electric Vehicle Charging Stations.
 
8. The applicant shall work with the County and the Utah Department of Transportation to 
create safe and usable trail access from the existing Rail Trail to the site.

9. The applicant shall provide a minimum of 5 Short-term bicycle parking spaces on site 
where bicycles may be securely stored, with both wheels resting on a stable surface and 
conveniently accessed and removed without requiring the movement of other bicycles, 
vehicles, or other objects. 
10. All mechanical equipment for the convenience store shall be screened from view from 
public roads. 
11. No signs are approved as part of this Conditional Use Permit. The applicant shall submit 
a sign application for review and approval prior to the installation of any signs.

Memorandum 
 
Date: 11/14/2022 
 
To: Ray Milliner 
 
From:  Carl Miller, PMP, AICP CTP 
  
Re:  Maverik Parking Reduction 
 
This memo is submitted in support of the applicant’s request to waive the maximum parking 
requirements for the proposed Maverik gas station near the Quinns Junction at US-40 
interchange and State Route 248. According to current zoning regulations, the maximum parking 
allocation is capped at 3.5 spaces per 1,000 square feet of leasable area, which translates to a 
maximum 21 parking spaces. However, Maverik is proposing 31 spaces—10 more than the 
allowable limit. 
 
While the maximum of 21 parking spaces is derived from the Institute of Transportation 
Engineers (ITE) Parking Manual, this assumes a typical amount of traffic for a convenience 
store. However, Maverik’s business model often attracts higher vehicular volumes in high-traffic 
locations like this one. The additional parking spaces will accommodate customer demand 
without straining roadways. 
 
Given the station’s proximity to the high-volume roads of Quinns Junction US-40 and State 
Route 248, adequate parking capacity is crucial to avoid overflow and queuing issues. A parking 
shortage could potentially lead to vehicles queuing onto Old Highway 40, posing a safety hazard 
for local motorists. 
 
 
Thank you for your consideration. 
 
 
 
Carl Miller, PMP, AICP CTP 
Transportation Planning Director

MEMORANDUM 
 
 
Date:  August 22, 2024 
To:  Planner for Clark County Planning Board 
From:  James Copeland, P.E. – AWA Engineering 
Subject: Justification for Requested Number of Parking Stalls 
 
 
This letter is a site-specific study to justify the number of parking stalls intended for the site. The ITE Parking 
Generation Manual – 5
th  Edition was used to provide an estimate of adequate parking for the site. 
 
The proposed development is a 5,951-square foot Maverik convenience store with 25 fueling positions (16 vehicle 
lanes and 9 commercial truck/RV lanes). The Maverik store will sell convenience items, snacks and beverages, and 
hot/prepared food. The proposed Maverik will cater to traveling customers and will have no overnight facilities or 
showers. The proposed use corresponds most closely with ITE land use 960: Super Convenience Market with Gas 
Station. 
 
Parking Generation – Super Convenience Market with Gas Station  
 
The Institute of Transportation Engineers (ITE) Parking Generation Manual (5th Edition) provides data regarding 
parking demand across various land use categories. For your reference, the section relevant to land use category 960 
has been included in the appendix. This section provides a comprehensive description of the land use category along 
with the corresponding calculated parking demand metrics. 
 
According to the ITE’s findings for land use 960, the weekday peak period parking demand averages 8.11 stalls per 
1,000 square feet of gross floor area (GFA). For the Saturday (weekend) peak period, the demand is slightly lower, 
averaging 5.07 stalls per 1,000 square feet of GFA. 
 
Using the ITE’s weekday peak period demand of 8.11 stalls per 1,000 square feet, we calculated that 48 parking stalls 
would be required during weekdays, with a reduced requirement of 30 stalls during weekends. This leads to an 
overall average estimated parking demand of 43 stalls. In line with the planning commission's requirement to reduce 
the parking count by seven stalls, the parking allocation has been adjusted to 31 stalls. 
 
This letter requests the planning commission to reconsider allowing Maverik to accommodate 37 stalls to facilitate 
anticipated parking on site.

Executive Summary 
 
I. Introduction and Summary 
 
A. Purpose of Report and Study Objectives 
 
The proposed development site, a 7.65 parcel, is located on the north side of SR-248 to the east of 
Frontage Road and south of Atkinson Road in Summit County, Utah. 
 
Summit County is currently in the process of completing final designs for the intersection of the Frontage 
Road and SR-248 to be relocated approximately 225 feet to the east of its current l ocation.   
 
This traffic study will look at the impacts of the proposed Maverik development.  
 
Figure One shows the location of the development. Trip projection and traffic analysis in this report will 
be performed for the AM and PM peak hours. 
 
B. Executive Summary 
 
For the SR-248 and Frontage Road intersection, the Synchro analysis shows that it will not operate at 
acceptable levels of service. The is because the southbound left turn will drop to LOS F during the peak 
hours with the site.  The signal warrant study found that the Peak Hour, Eight-Hour and Four -Hour signal 
warrants were met and that a traffic signal would operate efficiently.  
 
For the Maverik Access and the Frontage Road, the Synchro analysis shows that it will operate 
adequately with the proposed development. The Synchro analysis showed that the provided storage 
lengths are more than adequate for the projected queues. The left turn and right turn auxiliary lane checks 
showed that a northbound right turn lane would be required based on typical UDOT thresholds for a decel 
lane.  Similarly, a southbound left turn pocket would also be requ ired based on the typical UDOT 
thresholds. 
 
For Atkinson Road and the Frontage Road, the Synchro analysis shows that it will operate adequately 
with the proposed development. The Synchro analysis showed that the provided storage lengths are more 
than adequate for the projected queues. The left turn and right turn auxiliary lane checks showed that a 
northbound right turn lane would meet the typical UDOT thresholds for a decel lane.  The so uthbound 
traffic was not enough to meet the threshold, so a left turn pocket was not required. 
 
There is potential need for Maverik to contribute to the Frontage Road design elements or a potential 
traffic signal at SR-248 and the Frontage Road.  The study showed that new Maverik traffic contributed 
54 percent of the total traffic at the Maverik Access  with the Frontage Road.  For the SR-248 and 
Frontage Road signal, 12 percent of the traffic was related to new Maverik traffic.  
 
The developer is recommended to install off-site signing, striping and pavement markings in accordance 
with current Summit County specifications and the most current edition of the Manual on Uniform Traffic 
Control Devices (MUTCD).

II.  Proposed Development 
 
The proposed fuel station is to have a 5,951 square foot convenience store with 16 vehicle fueling 
positions, an additional 16 future fueling positions and 6 commercial truck / RV fueling positions, for a 
total of 38 fueling positions. 
 
Two accesses are planned for this site, Access A and Access B. Access A is proposed as a full movement 
access on the Frontage Road.  Access B is proposed as an entrance only, mainly for truck/vehicle 
separation.  Access B will be located on Atkinson Roa d.  
 
  
III.  Area Conditions 
 
Trip projection and traffic analysis in this report will be performed for the AM and PM peak hours.   
 
The intersection configuration at SR-248 and the Frontage Road is as follows:  
 
• SR-248, eastbound/westbound, has a dedicated left turn lane, and two through lanes.  The 
westbound traffic has a right turn deceleration lane. 
• Frontage Road, northbound/southbound has one lane in each direction with no center turn lane.  
At the intersection of SR-248, a southbound left turn pocket is avai lable. 
 
The speed limit on SR-248, is 55 mph. The speed limit on the Frontage Road is 40 mph.

A. Existing Traffic Data 
 
Intersection counts were made on Thursday, November 2, 2023, from 7:00 - 9:00 a.m. and 4:00 - 6:00 
p.m. The counts were made at the following intersections: 
 
• SR-248 and Frontage Road 
• Frontage Road and Atkinson Road 
 
The AM Peak Hour was from 7:45 – 8:45 a.m. The PM Peak Hour was from 4:15 - 5:15 p.m.   
 
Detailed counts are shown in the Appendix.  
 
Figure Three shows the existing traffic counts for the project site.    
 
B. 5-year traffic growth Projection 
 
The study parameters included traffic projections over the next five years, an annual growth rate of 
2% was used for a growth factor of 1.10.  This growth rate was based on reviewing the projected 
growth along SR-248.  Utah’s Unified Transportation Plan shows that on SR-248 to the east of the 
Frontage Road was 12,119 ADT in 2019 growing to 22,000 in 2050. This equated to a 1.95% growth 
rate or growth factor of 1.82 over 31 years. Therefore, the conservatively rounded 2% growth rate 
was used for future five-year projections. 
 
Figure Four shows the 2028 background traffic projected counts.

IV. Projected Traffic 
 
While the Institute of Transportation Engineers (ITE) Trip Generation (Eleventh Edition) handbook is 
typically used to estimate trips for land uses, AWA has transaction data for Maverik stores that are more 
accurate than ITE. In recent studies in Douglas, Wyoming and North Las Vegas, Nevada, it was found 
that the transaction data yielded trip rates that were conservative as compared to actual trip counts.   
 
The proposed land use utilized for this analysis is as follows: 
 
• Maverik Convenience Store – 36 fueling positions. 
 
The total transactions included fuel purchases (passenger and heavy vehicles) and in-store transactions. 
The total transactions were multiplied by 2.0 to estimate trips to/from the site. Total transactions times 2.0 
should be a conservative estimation of trips generated as it is more common for additional transactions 
(i.e., a fueling vehicle making separate transactions for fuel and food) than it is for less transactions (i.e., 
walk in purchases, no purchases after driving on the site, etc). 
 
Table One shows the locations that were sampled (eleven stores), fueling positions and trips generated 
based on total transactions times 2.0.  
 
The data shows what is expected, Maverik stores have a greater peak in the AM due to workers 
purchasing food. The transaction data also has a mix of heavy vehicles, which do not fuel as quickly as 
passenger car vehicles.  These blended fueling positions yield a lower trip ra te than passenger car vehicles 
only. 
 
Primary Trips  
 
Primary trips represent those trips which are not already passing by the site. These trips could be 
direct trips to the site from another location or diverted trips from other streets in the area. 
 
Pass-by Trips 
 
Pass-by trips were estimated.  Pass-by trips represent those trips which already pass the site as 
background traffic. This traffic already exists on the roadway and does not constitute additional 
traffic.  
 
ITE suggests that fuel centers such as this will have pass-by rates over 50 percent. In considering the 
major streets that this development will front, it is considered reasonable to assume a 50 percent pass-
by rate.

Pass-by Traffic 
 
For pass-by trips, it should be noted that the traffic already exists on the highway and is diverted to the 
new site. The diversions from the highway are repres ented with negative trips. The Maverik transactions 
show that approximately 10 percent of overall transactions are truck fueling , so 10 percent of pass-by 
traffic was projected to enter at Atkinson Road access.  The pass-by assumptions were based on the 
average of AM/PM traffic and are as follows:  
 
• 40% - Westbound right-in/right-out from SR-248 
• 35% - Eastbound left-in/left-out from SR-248 
• 25% - Frontage Road  
o 15% Northbound right-in/right-out 
o 10% Southbound left-in/left-out

V. Traffic Analysis 
 
The accesses and intersections were analyzed based on the Synchro software to evaluate the impacts of 
the project on the surrounding traffic network. As was discussed pre viously, the alternatives compare 
existing traffic and existing traffic with the proposed Maverik fuel station (with site).

B. Signal Warrant Analysis 
 
Warrant 3 – Peak Hour Volumes  
 
The volumes will be listed for both “AM/(PM)”. The traffic on SR-248 has a total eastbound volume of 
793/(803) and a westbound volume of 826/(887) at SR-248 and the Frontage Road.  This total two-way 
volume of 1,619/(1,690) vehicles per hour (Major Street) is one of the factors for determining whether a 
signal would be warranted for Warrant 3, Peak Hour Volume.   
 
As this volume far exceeds the threshold in for the Major Street of the Peak Hour Warrant, the Minor 
Street Volume must only exceed 100 vehicles per hour for a traffic signal to be warranted (see Figure 4C-
4 for Peak Hour Warrant on a Major Street above 40 mph).  The following page shows a plot of the Peak 
Hour Warrant based on MUTCD Figure 4C-4.  The projected southbound left turn traffic at the proposed 
signal would exceed Warrant 3 requirements, Peak Hour Volume. These volumes projected for the 
southbound left turns is: 170/(139) for the fuel center traffic. Therefore, the peak hour warrant will be 
met. 
 
Based on the above projections, Warrant 3 would be met.  It should be noted that it is currently 
near meeting Peak Hour Warrants without the Maverik projected traffic. 
 
 
AM - Major Street – 1,619 
Minor Street - 170 
PM - Major Street – 1,690 
Minor Street - 139

UDOT (US-40/SR-248 east side, signal performance metrics from 11/22/23) and from Summit County 
for the Frontage Road (12/30/2020).  This data was developed to show how other hours of the day 
compare to the peak hour of fuel center traffic. Only the percentage of peak hour from these counts, 
applied to the volumes in the traffic study, will be used for signal warrant analysis. These counts show 
that traffic is fairly consistent throughout the day.

Warrant 1 – Eight-Hour Vehicular Volume 
 
For the Eight-Hour Vehicular Volume warrant, Condition B – Interruption of Continuous Traffic, 630 
vehicles per hour on SR-248 (total of both directions) is required for the eight-hour period. This part of 
the warrant is projected at 1,487 vehicles (1,690 x 0.88), so well over the threshold.  
 
To meet the eight-hour warrant Condition B, the proposed signal would have to maintain at least 70 
southbound left turns per hour during the lowest volume of the highest 4 hours. This would yield 76 (90 x 
0.84) for the 2028 background without the site. The site is projected to add another 81 (96 x 0.84). This 
would yield a projected total of 157 minor street left turns for the eight-hour warrant. Therefore, both 
thresholds are met. 
 
Based on the above projections, Warrant 1 would be met without the Maverik, and exceeded with 
the proposed Maverik. 
63
 
23 
 
Warrant 2 – Four-Hour Vehicular Volume 
 
The next figure shows the MUTCD Figure 4C-2 for the four-hour vehicle warrant. While counts were not 
made for all hours, the major street volume was found to be 92 percent of the peak hour volume, based on 
UDOT counts for the same day at US-40/SR-248 east side. For the Frontage Road, the minor street 
volume was found to be 90 percent of the Frontage Road counts provided by Summit County.  
 
To meet the four-hour warrant, the proposed signal would have to maintain at least 80 southbound left 
turns per hour during the lowest volume of the highest 4 hours. This would yield 81 (90 x 0.90) for the 
2028 background without the site. The site is projected to add another 79 (88 x 0.90). This would yield a 
projected total of 159 minor street left turns for the four-hour warrant. Therefore, both thresholds are met 
as can be seen in the nomograph below. 
 
Warrant 2 – Four-Hour Vehicular Volume 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
(1) The four-hour major street volume was estimated as a minimum of 92 percent of the peak hour volume (1,690), 
based on UDOT counts for the same day. 
(2) The minor street volume was estimated based on 90 percent of both the 2028 background traffic plus 90 percent of 
the Maverik generated traffic.  
 
Based on the above projections, Warrant 2 would be met without the Maverik, and exceeded with 
the proposed Maverik. 
 
Major Street – 1,554(1) 
Minor Street – 159(2) 
64
 
24 
 
Warrant 7 – Crash Experience 
 
The crash data showed that six angle related crashes or nearly 66 percent of the crashes at this intersection 
were angle related from 2019 - 2022. Five crashes that could be resolved by a signal, in a year, is the 
threshold to meet Warrant 7 - Crash Experience warrant in MUTCD. In 2021, there were three angle 
related crashes. These crashes could increase with the p roposed development. 
 
Based on the accident data, Warrant 7 would not be met. 
 
Based on the signal warrant discussion and projections in this addendum, the following signal warrants 
were shown to be met: 
 
Warrant 3 – Peak Hour Volumes 
Warrant 2 – Four-Hour Vehicular Volume 
Warrant 1 – Eight-Hour Vehicular Volume 
 
C. Frontage Road Design Data 
 
The traffic impact study used the UDOT threshold of 10 left turns per hour to determine if a southbound 
left turn pocket was needed along the Frontage Road. Based on this threshold (51 projected southbound 
left turns), a southbound left turn pocket is needed at the Maverik access.  With th e need for a southbound 
left turn pocket at SR-248, then it is reasonable to extend the center left turn lane from the Maverik access 
to SR-248.  Based on the same threshold, a southbound left turn pocket at Atkinson Road was not needed 
(7 projected southbound left turns). 
 
For northbound right turns into the Maverik access, the UDOT threshold of 25 right turns was used to 
determine if there was a need for a deceleration lane.  The study showed 185 right turns, so a deceleration 
lane would be needed. Based on the same threshold a deceleration lane would also be needed at A tkinson 
Road due to the exiting traffic (total projected traffic is 66 of which 47 exist and 19 are projected from the 
Maverik). 
 
D. Maverik Traffic Percentages 
 
To project possible contributions to the design features of the re located Frontage Road and a potential 
signal at the Frontage Road and SR-248, the percent of Maverik traffic was calculated.  
 
For the Maverik Access to the Frontage Road, it was calculated that 54 percent of the projected traffic 
will be new Maverik traffic related. 
 
For the SR-248 and Frontage Road intersection, it was calculated that 12 percent of the traffic was 
projected to be new Maverik traffic. 
 
 
65
 
25 
 
 
VI. Conclusions/Recommendations 
 
For the SR-248 and Frontage Road intersection, the Synchro analysis shows that it will not operate at 
acceptable levels of service. The is because the southbound left turn will drop to LOS F during the peak 
hours with the site.  The signal warrant study found that the Peak Hour, Eight-Hour and Four -Hour signal 
warrants were met and that a traffic signal would operate efficiently.  
 
For the Maverik Access and the Frontage Road, the Synchro analysis shows that it will operate 
adequately with the proposed development. The Synchro analysis showed that the provided storage 
lengths are more than adequate for the projected queues. The left turn and right turn auxiliary lane checks 
showed that a northbound right turn lane would be required based on typical UDOT thresholds for a decel 
lane.  Similarly, a southbound left turn pocket would also be requ ired based on the typical UDOT 
thresholds. 
 
For Atkinson Road and the Frontage Road, the Synchro analysis shows that it will operate adequately 
with the proposed development. The Synchro analysis showed that the provided storage lengths are more 
than adequate for the projected queues. The left turn and right turn auxiliary lane checks showed that a 
northbound right turn lane would meet the typical UDOT thresholds for a decel lane.  The so uthbound 
traffic was not enough to meet the threshold, so a left turn pocket was not required. 
 
There is potential need for Maverik to contribute to the Frontage Road design elements or a potential 
traffic signal at SR-248 and the Frontage Road.  The study showed that new Maverik traffic contributed 
54 percent of the total traffic at the Maverik Access  with the Frontage Road.  For the SR-248 and 
Frontage Road signal, 12 percent of the traffic was related to new Maverik traffic.  
 
The developer is recommended to install off-site signing, striping and pavement markings in accordance 
with current Summit County specifications and the most current edition of the Manual on Uniform Traffic 
Control Devices (MUTCD).

Phase I Environmental Site Assessment
Maverik Big Hoss Property | Park City, Utah
January 10, 2024 | Terracon Project No. 61237408
Facilities  |   Environmental |  Geotechnical  |  Materials i
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
This Phase I Environmental Site Assessment (ESA) was performed in accordance with
our Task Order dated December 21, 2023, and was conducted consistent with the
procedures included in ASTM E1527-21, Standard Practice for Environmental Site
Assessments: Phase I Environmental Site Assessment Process . The purpose of this ESA
was to assist the client in developing information to identify Recognized Environmental
Conditions (RECs) in connection with the site as reflected by the scope of this report.
The ESA was conducted under the supervision or responsible charge of Tina Cheney,
Environmental Professional, who performed the site reconnaissance on January 5, 2024.
Findings and Opinions
A summary of findings is provided below. It should be recognized that details were not
included or fully developed in this section, and the report must be read in its entirety for
a comprehensive understanding of the items contained herein.
Site Description and Use
The site consists of 7.68-acre undeveloped lot. Approximately 3.5 acres of the east
portion of the site is considered wetlands and is not developable.
Historical Information
Based on a review of readily available historical documents, the site has consisted of
undeveloped land throughout its history. The adjoining properties to the north consisted
of undeveloped land until it was developed with a power substation by 1978. The north
adjoining properties were undergoing development in 1983 and were developed with an
industrial property by 1993. An additional industrial property utilized for equipment
storage was developed to the northeast by 1997. The east adjoining property has
consisted of a railway followed by vacant land since at least 1900 to the 1980s, and then
the railroad was removed and is now a trail. The southern adjoining property was
developed with a roadway by 1953, and the roadway was reworked and expanded by
1993. The western adjoining property was developed with an industrial property by
1976. An automotive repair facility has occupied the western adjoining property since at
least 2014; however, reports of environmental concerns associated with the property
were not identified. Based on the historical research, the use of the site and adjoining
properties does not represent a REC to the site.
Previous Reports
A Limited Site Investigation (LSI) Report dated October 26, 2021, and revised March 22,
2022, and a Response Action Report dated September 28, 2022, were identified for the
112
Phase I Environmental Site Assessment
Maverik Big Hoss Property | Park City, Utah
January 10, 2024 | Terracon Project No. 61237408
Facilities  |   Environmental |  Geotechnical  |  Materials ii
site. The Terracon LSI was conducted to evaluate the site for potential elevated
concentrations of metals as required by the United States Environmental Protection
Agency (EPA) due to the site’s location within the historical Richardson Flat Superfund
Site Operable Unit 2 boundary. Soil samples were collected from the top six inches of
soil throughout the site and one 0.24-acre area was identified as having arsenic and lead
concentrations exceeding the Interim Residential Land Use Action Levels. Terracon
conducted response actions in 2022, as detailed in the 2022 Response Action Report.
Shallow soils in the impacted area identified during the LSI were excavated to a depth of
six to eight inches below ground surface and transported off-site for disposal. Laboratory
analysis results of composite soil samples collected following the excavation did not
exceed action levels for lead or arsenic. Based on the remedial work performed, the
previous presence of lead and arsenic exceeding action levels represents a Historical
REC (HREC) to the site.
The wetland area is managed through an environmental covenant that outlines
continuing obligations and limitations associated with the property, which is considered a
Controlled REC (CREC).
Records Review
The site was not identified in the regulatory database report; however, the site is known
to be within the Richardson Flat CERCLA National Priorities List, Operable Unit 2, based
on local area knowledge. The Richardson Flat Tailings property includes the tailing ponds
area and Silver Creek, where tailings have accumulated. Lead and arsenic-impacted soil
have been identified in the surrounding floodplain. Sampling at the site was conducted
and discussed in the Previous Reports Section .
The facilities identified in the database report do not appear to represent a REC to the
site at this time based on regulatory status, apparent topographic gradient, and/or
distance from the site.
Site Reconnaissance
During the site reconnaissance minor amounts of trash and debris were observed. An
area of the site had clearly been excavated, which is discussed in Section 3.7. A wetland
area was observed on the east side of the site. RECs were not identified during the site
reconnaissance.
Adjoining Properties
North of the site is the Fire Department, Republic Services, and vacant land. East of the
site is vacant land. South of the site is vacant land, Old Highway 40, and Kearns
Boulevard. West of the site is Atkinson Road followed by Geber Collision & Glass. RECs
were not identified with the adjoining properties.
113
Phase I Environmental Site Assessment
Maverik Big Hoss Property | Park City, Utah
January 10, 2024 | Terracon Project No. 61237408
Facilities  |   Environmental |  Geotechnical  |  Materials iii
Significant Data Gaps
No significant data gaps were identified.
Conclusions
We have performed a Phase I ESA consistent with the procedures included in ASTM
Practice E1527-21 at Atkinson Avenue & Old Highway 40, Park City, Summit County,
Utah, the site. The following CREC was identified in connection with the site:
n The site is located within Richardson Flat NPL Operable Unit 2. The west side of
the site is acceptable for commercial use; however, the wetland area is managed
through an environmental covenant that outlines continuing obligations and
limitations associated with the property, which is considered a CREC.
Recommendations
Based on the scope of services, limitations, and conclusions of this assessment, Terracon
recommends that the client ensure that development of the site complies with the
environmental covenant.

Reason for the ESA Acquiring the site
The location of the site is depicted on Exhibit 1 of Appendix A, which was reproduced
from a portion of the United States Geological Survey (USGS) 7.5-minute series
topographic map. The site and adjoining properties are depicted on the Site Diagram,
which is included as Exhibit 2 of Appendix A. Acronyms and terms used in this report are
described in Appendix F.
1.2 Scope of Services
This Phase I Environmental Site Assessment (ESA) was performed in accordance with
our Task Order dated December 21, 2023, and was conducted consistent with the
procedures included in ASTM E1527-21, Standard Practice for Environmental Site
Assessments: Phase I Environmental Site Assessment Process . The purpose of this ESA
was to assist the client in developing information to identify Recognized Environmental
Conditions (RECs) in connection with the site as reflected by the scope of this report.
RECs are defined by ASTM E1527-21 as “(1) the presence of hazardous substances or
petroleum products in, on, or at the subject property due to a release to the
environment; (2) the likely presence of hazardous substances or petroleum products in,
on, or at the subject property due to a release or likely release to the environment; or
(3) the presence of hazardous substances or petroleum products in, on, or at the subject
property under conditions that pose a material threat of a future release to the
environment.” A de minimis condition is not a recognized environmental condition.
This purpose was undertaken through user-provided information, a regulatory database
review, historical and physical records review, interviews (including local government
inquiries, as applicable), and a visual noninvasive reconnaissance of the site and
adjoining properties. Limitations, ASTM deviations, and significant data gaps (if
identified) are noted in the applicable sections of the report.

1.3 Standard of Care
This ESA was performed in accordance with generally accepted practices of this
profession, undertaken in similar studies at the same time and in the same geographical
area. We have endeavored to meet this standard of care, but may be limited by
conditions encountered during performance, a client-driven scope of work, or inability to
review information not received by the report date. Where appropriate, these limitations
are discussed in the text of the report, and an evaluation of their significance with
respect to our findings has been conducted.
Phase I ESAs, such as the one performed at this site, are of limited scope, are
noninvasive, and cannot eliminate the potential that hazardous, toxic, or petroleum
substances are present or have been released at the site beyond what is identified by
the limited scope of this ESA. In conducting the limited scope of services described
herein, certain sources of information and public records were not reviewed. It should be
recognized that environmental concerns may be documented in public records that were
not reviewed. No ESA can wholly eliminate uncertainty regarding the potential for RECs
in connection with a property. Performance of this practice is intended to reduce, but not
eliminate, uncertainty regarding the potential for RECs. No warranties, express or
implied, are intended or made. The limitations herein must be considered when the user
of this report formulates opinions as to risks associated with the site or otherwise uses
the report for any other purpose. These risks may be further evaluated—but not
eliminated—through additional research or assessment. We will, upon request, advise
you of additional research or assessment options that may be available and associated
costs.
1.4 Additional Scope Limitations, ASTM Deviations, and Data
Gaps
Based upon the agreed-on scope of services, this ESA did not include subsurface or
other invasive assessments, vapor intrusion assessments or indoor air quality
assessments (i.e., evaluation of the presence of vapors within a building structure),
business environmental risk evaluations, or other services not particularly identified and
discussed herein. Credentials of the company (Statement of Qualifications) have not
been included in this report but are available upon request. Pertinent documents are
referred to in the text of this report, and a separate reference section has not been
included. Reasonable attempts were made to obtain information within the scope and
time constraints set forth by the client; however, in some instances, information
requested is not, or was not, received by the issuance date of the report. Information
obtained for this ESA was received from several sources that we believe to be reliable;
nonetheless, the authenticity or reliability of these sources cannot and is not warranted
hereunder. This ESA was further limited by the following:

Environmental lien and activity and use limitation (AUL) records recorded against the

notified otherwise, we assume that the client is evaluating this information outside the
scope of this report.

State Engineering Control (EC) and Institutional Control (IC) databases. Based on a
review of the database report, the site was not listed on the EC or IC databases. Please
note that in addition to these federal and state listings, AULs can be recorded at the
county and municipal level that may not be listed in the regulatory database report.

3.7 Prior Report Review

n Limited Site Investigation Report
Big Hoss Property
Dated: October 26, 2021, revised March 22, 2022
Prepared by: Terracon
For: Big Hoss Properties, LLC
n Response Action Report
Big Hoss Property
Dated: September 28, 2022
Prepared by: Terracon
For: Big Hoss Properties, LLC
The Terracon 2021 LSI was conducted to evaluate the site for potential elevated
concentrations of metals as required by the EPA due to the site’s location within the
historical Richardson Flat Superfund Site Operable Unit 2 boundary. Soil samples were
collected from the top six inches of soil throughout the site and one 0.24-acre area was
identified as having arsenic and lead concentrations exceeding the Interim Residential
Land Use Action Levels. Terracon conducted response actions in 2022, as detailed in the
2022 Response Action Report. Shallow soils in the impacted area identified during the
LSI were excavated to a depth of six to eight inches below ground surface and
transported off-site for disposal. Laboratory analysis results of composite soil samples
collected following the excavation did not exceed action levels for lead or arsenic. Based
on the remedial work performed, the previous presence of lead and arsenic exceeding
action levels represents a Historical REC (HREC) to the site.

The wetland area is managed through an environmental covenant that outlines
continuing obligations and limitations associated with the property, which is considered a
Controlled REC.
4.0 RECORDS REVIEW
Regulatory database information was provided by EDR, a contract information services
company in a report dated December 28, 2023. The purpose of the records review was
to identify RECs in connection with the site. Information in this section is subject to the
accuracy of the data provided by the information services company and the date at
which the information is updated. The scope herein did not include confirmation of
facilities listed as "unmappable" by regulatory databases.
In some of the following subsections, the words up-gradient, cross-gradient, and down-
gradient refer to the topographic gradient in relation to the site. As stated previously,
the groundwater flow direction and the depth to shallow groundwater, if present, would
likely vary depending upon seasonal variations in rainfall and the depth to the
soil/bedrock interface. Without the benefit of on-site groundwater monitoring wells
surveyed to a datum, groundwater depth and flow direction beneath the site cannot be
directly ascertained.

Gerber Collision & Glass
This facility, located northwest adjacent and cross-gradient of the site, was identified on
the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) Very Small Quantity Generators
(VQSG) database for the handling of ignitable waste and various spent nonhalogenated
solvents. The facility is regulated as a conditionally exempt small quantity generator and
records of violations were not identified. Based on regulatory status and the small
quantity generated, this listing does not indicate a REC to the site.
Park City Service Center
This facility, previously located northwest adjacent and cross-gradient of the site, was
identified on the Underground Storage Tank (UST) and Leaking UST (LUST) databases.
Four USTs are reported to have previously existed at this facility, two installed in March
1976 and removed in May 1991 and two installed in July 1991 and removed in June
1997. A spill report was submitted to the Utah Department of Environmental Quality
(UDEQ) on March 15, 1990 due to a tightness test failure on the fill pipes connected to
the USTs. The leak was reported to be repaired when the fill pipes were tightened on
March 30, 1991 and the USTs were removed in May 1991 to prevent potential future
releases. Laboratory analysis of soil samples collected from the UST basin indicated
petroleum contamination. Excavation activities continued until total petroleum
hydrocarbons were detected at concentrations less than 100 parts per million and were
completed on June 27, 1991. Contaminated soils were exported from the facility for
proper disposal. The UDEQ issued a No Further Action letter on April 12, 1995. Based on
the corrective actions performed, regulatory status, and topographic gradient, this listing
does not indicate a REC to the site.
The remaining facilities listed in the database report do not appear to represent RECs to
the site at this time based upon regulatory status, apparent topographic gradient,
and/or distance from the site.
Unmapped facilities are those that do not contain sufficient address or location
information to evaluate the facility listing locations relative to the site. The report listed

EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
DESCRIPTION
Environmental Data Resources, Inc.’s (EDR) City Directory Report is a screening tool designed to assist 
environmental professionals in evaluating potential liability on a target property resulting from past 
activities.EDR’s City Directory Report includes a search of available business directory data at 
approximately five year intervals.
RECORD SOURCES
The EDR City Directory Report accesses a variety of business directory sources, including Haines, InfoUSA, 
Po lk,Cole, Bresser, and Stewart. Listings marked as EDR Digital Archive access Cole and InfoUSA records. 
The various directory sources enhance and complement each other to provide a more thorough and 
accurate report.
EDR is licensed to reproduce certain City Directory works by the copyright holders of those works. The 
purchaser of this EDR City Directory Report may include it in report(s) delivered to a customer.

 INTRODUCTION
The property owner, Big Hoss Properties LLC, is in the process of planning the development of
the subject property (Exhibit 1). The property is included in the historic Richardson Flat
Superfund Site Operable Unit 2 (OU2) boundaries, where lead and arsenic impacted soil have
been identified in the surrounding flood plain.  Site characterization is required to evaluate the
upland soils for potential elevated concentrations of metals as per the requirements set forth in
Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) Administrative Settlement Agreement and Order on
Consent (AOC) Richardson Flat Tailings Site Park City, Utah, Operable Units 2 and 3 and
Summit County, dated March 17, 2021.
Terracon prepared a Sampling and Analysis Plan / Quality Assurance Project Plan (SAP/QAPP;
September 8, 2021) designed to follow the Statement of Work for Development (SOW) detailed
in Appendix A of the AOC. The SAP/QAPP was reviewed and approved by EPA Region 8 and
the Utah Department of Environmental Quality (UDEQ), respectively (“the Agencies.”)
1.1 Site Description and Background
The subject property is currently undeveloped. The proposed scope of work detailed in the
SAP/QAPP followed the SOW detailed in Appendix A of the AOC and was intended to gather
the necessary data to evaluate whether elevated concentrations of the contaminants of concern
(COCs) have impacted soils on the property.  It is anticipated that the property will be developed
for commercial use.
Of the 7.56-acre property, approximately 3.49 acres are considered wetland and required
wetland set-back based on a land use evaluation survey conducted by Dominion Engineering
(Exhibit 2) and are excluded from developable land. The wetland area will be managed through
an Environmental Covenant that outlines the continuing obligations and limitations associated
with the property and will be recorded on the Title to the land. The surveyed property
boundaries, excluding the wetland and wetland setback, were used to generate a sampling grid.
Based on an initial site visit, no areas of exposed tailings or features such as ditches were
identified in the investigation area.
1.2 Regulatory Standards and Criteria
The laboratory analytical results for lead and arsenic were compared to the current site specific,
interim residential land use action levels for arsenic (19 mg/kg) and lead (260 mg/kg)
established in Appendix A of the AOC. All other analytes were evaluated using the May 2021
EPA Regional Screening Levels (RSLs) for residential and industrial soil.
197
Limited Site Investigation Report; Revision 1
Big Hoss Property ■ Summit County, Utah
March 22, 2022 ■ Terracon Project No. 61217135
Responsive ■ Resourceful ■ Reliable 2
 SITE INVESTIGATION
2.1 Sampling Process Design
The sampling program detailed in the SAP was developed to address the requirement to
characterize upland soils on the site following the SOW detailed in AOC.  As defined in the
SOW, the following sampling protocol has been identified to properly characterize soils at the
site: Initial property characterization (sampling) shall include decision units on the property not
to exceed 0.25 acres . As stated in the SOW, “ In accordance with the Summit County
Snyderville Basin General Plan and Federal Emergency Management Agency flood plain maps,
no areas identified in a wetland or floodplain may be developed or disturbed and Settling Party
is not required to characterize or remediate any such portions of the Property as part of this
Scope of Work or in conjunction with the AOC.”
Based on the sampling frequency defined in the SOW, 16 decision units required sampling.
Decision units are shown on Exhibit 3.
2.2 Field Data Collection
Terracon conducted the field work on September 14, 2021. Test pits were installed with a
backhoe and performed in such a manner as to minimize fugitive emissions. Samples were
collected following applicable Terracon Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) and the
methodology defined the SAP, as detailed below. Sampling locations are shown on Exhibit 4.
a. One five-point composite soil sample was collected from each decision unit from a depth
of 0 to 6 inches. Single-use disposable hand-tools were used to collect the soil samples
from a freshly-exposed sidewall of each test pit. Each composite soil sample consisted
of five (5) subsamples collected from five (5) separate and distinct locations within each
decision unit. Each composite sample was thoroughly homogenized; large debris and
rocks were removed prior to homogenization of soil.
b. Depth-profile samples were collected from a subset of three (3) decision units, SS-5, SS-
8, and SS-14. Depth-profiling samples were collected from three depths: 0 to 6 inches, 6
to 12 inches, and 12 to 18 inches.
c. A total of twenty-two (22) soil samples were collected: sixteen (16) composite soil
samples from the 0 to 6 inch soil profile; three (3) composite soil samples from the 6 to
12 inch soil profile, and three (3) composite soil samples from the 12 to 18 inch soil
profile.
The samples were shipped in iced coolers under chain-of-custody protocols via overnight
courier to Pace Analytical Laboratories in Mt. Juliet, Tennessee (a Utah-Certified Laboratory).
198
Limited Site Investigation Report; Revision 1
Big Hoss Property ■ Summit County, Utah

Responsive ■ Resourceful ■ Reliable 3
2.3 Field QA/QC Samples
Two field duplicate samples were collected: “DUP-1” was collected from decision unit #9 from
the 0 to 6 inch interval; “DUP-2” was collected from decision unit #14 from the 6 to 12 inch
interval.  The duplicate samples were submitted for the same analyses as the decision unit
samples.
 LABORATORY ANALYTICAL PROGRAM
All soil samples were analyzed for the “Inorganic Analytical Service Target Analytes”, excluding
cyanide, as defined in the SOW.  The samples were analyzed using EPA Method 6020, with the
exception of mercury, which was analyzed using EPA Method 7471A.  All analytical methods
followed standard EPA procedures as outlined in Test Methods for Evaluating Solid Wastes -
Physical/Chemical Methods (SW-846).
 QUALITY ASSURANCE / QUALITY CONTROL (QA/QC)
Quality assurance / quality control for the sampling program included using standardized
sample collection and handling methods, documenting pertinent field information, and keeping
chain-of-custody records. All sampling was conducted following Terracon’s SOPs.
Sample analyses was conducted by Pace Analytical Laboratories (Pace), a Utah-certified
environmental laboratory. Pace followed its prescribed Quality Assurance Project Plan (QAPP),
and the data package generated by Pace included quality control and quality assurance
analyses and procedures. A copy of the laboratory analytical report is included in Appendix C.
Intra-laboratory precision was assessed by the comparison of soil samples and duplicate field
soil samples.
 RESULTS
Obvious indications of mining waste materials (e.g., yellow-stained soils or slag materials) were
not observed in the test pits. Soil sample results were compared to the Interim Residential Land
Use action levels for arsenic (19 mg/kg) and lead (260 mg/kg) established in Appendix A of the
AOC. All other analytes were evaluated using the May 2021 EPA Regional Screening Levels
(RSLs) for residential and industrial soil.
199
Limited Site Investigation Report; Revision 1
Big Hoss Property ■ Summit County, Utah
March 22, 2022 ■ Terracon Project No. 61217135
Responsive ■ Resourceful ■ Reliable 4
5.1 Soil Sample Analytical Results
One soil sample, SS-3 @ 0 to 6 inches, exceeded the Interim Residential Land Use Action
Levels for both lead and arsenic. The concentrations reported, 31.8 mg/kg arsenic and 270
mg/kg lead, exceeded the action levels of 19 mg/kg and 260 mg/kg, respectively. No other soil
samples exceeded an action level or screening level.
5.2 Data Quality Assessment
The purpose of data quality assessment is to ensure that data generated under the QA/QC
Program is reconciled, accurate, and consistent with program data quality objectives (DQOs).
DQOs are expressed in terms of precision, accuracy, representativeness, comparability,
completeness, and sensitivity (PARCCS).
 Precision
Precision is the degree of agreement among repeated measurements and is quantitatively
measured. Laboratory analysis precision was determined by calculating the Relative Percent
Difference (RPD) between the laboratory sample and the field duplicate. The RPD objective was
35% or less. Table 2 of Appendix B displays the RPD data.
Field duplicates DUP-1 and DUP-2 (primary samples SS-9@0-6 and SS-14@6-12,
respectively) were analyzed for metals and mercury. The RPDs were within acceptable QC
limits; therefore, qualification is not necessary except for the Cobalt in DUP-1. The ROD does
not consider cobalt to be an analyte of concern to the site. In Terracon’s opinion, the cobalt RPD
exceedance does not impact the decision.
 Accuracy
Accuracy is a measure of confidence in a result (i.e., a determination of how close the
measurement is to the true value) and was assessed via percent recovery in matrix spike,
matrix spike duplicate, and laboratory control sample analyses.
QC batch WG1742449-03 used sample L1404521-03 as the original sample in the MS/MSD
analysis. The MS/MSD was analyzed to identify the interaction of the sample matrix and
mercury. The percent recovery was within QC limits; therefore, no qualification of the data is
necessary. MS/MSD was performed on QC batches: WG1742444, WG1742994, WG1742383,
WG1742385 however, the original samples used to analyze the MS/MSD are from another site;
therefore, the MS/MSDs cannot be used to assess matrix effects.
Laboratory control samples (LCS) were analyzed in SDG L1404521.The percent recoveries for
the LCS were within acceptable QC limits; therefore, no qualification of the data is necessary.
Ten method blanks (R3707197-1, R3707441-1, R3707372-1, R3706249-1, R3706792-1,
R3706703-1, R3706500-1, R3706505-2, R3706495-1, R3706506-1) were analyzed for the
200
Limited Site Investigation Report; Revision 1
Big Hoss Property ■ Summit County, Utah
March 22, 2022 ■ Terracon Project No. 61217135
Responsive ■ Resourceful ■ Reliable 5
samples in SDG L1404521. No compounds were detected except for selenium in QC batch
WG1742385. Samples SS-3, SS-12, SS-13, SS-14, SS-15, SS-23 were qualified in the
laboratory report with a B. Selenium in the above samples did not exceed the RDL. In spite of
bias, the decision is intact.
 Representativeness
Representativeness is the extent to which measurements depict the true environmental
condition being evaluated. Representativeness was assessed by verifying samples were
analyzed within their analytical method holding times and that method blanks do not report any
detections. The samples were deemed Representative. A review of the laboratory analytical
reports indicated all sample analyses was performed within holding times, the samples were
analyzed following the correct methods, all instruments and calibration check requirements were
met, and the method blanks and Laboratory Control Samples met batch QC requirements. The
laboratory case narrative did not recommend corrective action.
Samples were shipped to Pace Analytical National, Mount Juliet, Tennessee. The laboratory
case narrative noted that all holding times and temperatures were within QC limits and that all
samples were in good condition.
 Comparability
Comparability is the extent to which data from one study can be compared directly to either past
data from the current project or data from another study. For this project, comparability was
assessed by the units of measure reported in the analytical results compared to the units of
measure established for Action Levels and by using analytical methods that are comparable to
other similar studies in the same area and under the same regulatory framework.  The data was
deemed Comparable.
Based on Terracon’s review of the analytical data and associated data quality assessment
findings, we believe that the data quality is sufficient for the intended purpose of characterizing
the soils.
 Completeness
Completeness is the measure of the quantity of valid data obtained from a measurement system
compared to the quantity that was expected under normal conditions. Although a completeness
goal of 100 percent is desirable, an overall completeness goal of 80 to 90 percent may be
realistically achieved under normal field sampling and laboratory analysis conditions.
The QAPP defined sixteen composite soil samples from 0 to 6 inches, three composite soil
samples from 6 to 12 inches, three composite soil samples from 12 to 18 inches, and two blind
duplicates from various locations and depths. According to the COC and based on the results
produced by the laboratory, a total of 24 samples were received and analyzed; therefore, the
sample completeness is 100 percent.
201
Limited Site Investigation Report; Revision 1
Big Hoss Property ■ Summit County, Utah
March 22, 2022 ■ Terracon Project No. 61217135
Responsive ■ Resourceful ■ Reliable 6
The laboratory received 22 composite soil samples and two blind duplicates to be analyzed. The
laboratory produced 100 percent usable critical data.
 Sensitivity
Sensitivity is the ability of a method or instrument to detect target compounds at the level of
interest. Sample dilutions due to limited recovery and/or gross contamination may be required to
produce quantifiable results and prevent equipment damage. Such dilutions may result in
detection limits above target levels. The significance of elevated detections or quantification
limits, if any, are evaluated on a case-by-case basis.
The method detection limits for all analytes were below EPA RSL values, respectively;
therefore, no qualification of the data is necessary.
 CONCLUSIONS
Based on the results of this investigation, shallow soils within one decision unit, SS-3, slightly
exceeded the Interim Residential Land Use Action Levels for total lead and arsenic.  The
concentration of lead reported, 270 mg/kg, did not exceed the EPA RSL for residential soil of
400 mg/kg lead. Soils in this area will be addressed through a Response Action and Soil
Management Plan.
As stated in Section 1.1, the wetland area will be managed through an Environmental Covenant
that outlines the continuing obligations and limitations associated with the property and will be
recorded on the Title to the land.

CASE NARRATIVE
Unless qualified or notated within the narrative below, all sample aliquots were received at the correct 
temperature, in the proper containers, with the appropriate preservatives, and within method specified 
holding times.  Where applicable, all MDL (LOD) and RDL (LOQ) values reported for environmental 
samples have been corrected for the dilution factor used in the analysis.  All Method and Batch Quality 
Control are within established criteria except where addressed in this case narrative, a 
non-conformance form or properly qualified within the sample results. By my digital signature below, I 
affirm to the best of my knowledge, all problems/anomalies observed by the laboratory as having the 
potential to affect the quality of the data have been identified by the laboratory, and no information or 
data have been knowingly withheld that would affect the quality of the data.
[Preliminary Report]

GLOSSARY OF TERMS
Guide to Reading and Understanding Your Laboratory Report
The information below is designed to better explain the various terms used in your report of analytical results from the Laboratory.  This is not 
intended as a comprehensive explanation, and if you have additional questions please contact your project representative.
Results Disclaimer - Information that may be provided by the customer, and contained within this report, include Permit Limits, Project Name, 
Sample ID, Sample Matrix, Sample Preservation, Field Blanks, Field Spikes, Field Duplicates, On-Site Data, Sampling Collection Dates/Times, and 
Sampling Location. Results relate to the accuracy of this information provided, and as the samples are received.
Abbreviations and Definitions
(dry) Results are reported based on the dry weight of the sample. [this will only be present on a dry report basis for soils].
MDL Method Detection Limit.
MDL (dry) Method Detection Limit.
RDL Reported Detection Limit.
RDL (dry) Reported Detection Limit.
Rec. Recovery.
RPD Relative Percent Difference.
SDG Sample Delivery Group.
U Not detected at the Reporting Limit (or MDL where applicable).
Analyte The name of the particular compound or analysis performed. Some Analyses and Methods will have multiple analytes 
reported.
Dilution
If the sample matrix contains an interfering material, the sample preparation volume or weight values differ from the 
standard, or if concentrations of analytes in the sample are higher than the highest limit of concentration that the 
laboratory can accurately report, the sample may be diluted for analysis. If a value different than 1 is used in this field, the 
result reported has already been corrected for this factor.
Limits
These are the target % recovery ranges or % difference value that the laboratory has historically determined as normal 
for the method and analyte being reported. Successful QC Sample analysis will target all analytes recovered or 
duplicated within these ranges.
Original Sample The non-spiked sample in the prep batch used to determine the Relative Percent Difference (RPD) from a quality control 
sample. The Original Sample may not be included within the reported SDG.
Qualifier
This column provides a letter and/or number designation that corresponds to additional information concerning the result
reported. If a Qualifier is present, a definition per Qualifier is provided within the Glossary and Definitions page and 
potentially a discussion of possible implications of the Qualifier in the Case Narrative if applicable.
Result
The actual analytical final result (corrected for any sample specific characteristics) reported for your sample. If there was 
no measurable result returned for a specific analyte, the result in this column may state “ND” (Not Detected) or “BDL” 
(Below Detectable Levels). The information in the results column should always be accompanied by either an MDL 
(Method Detection Limit) or RDL (Reporting Detection Limit) that defines the lowest value that the laboratory could detect 
or report for this analyte.
Uncertainty 
(Radiochemistry) Confidence level of 2 sigma.
Case Narrative (Cn)
A brief discussion about the included sample results, including a discussion of any non-conformances to protocol 
observed either at sample receipt by the laboratory from the field or during the analytical process. If present, there will 
be a section in the Case Narrative to discuss the meaning of any data qualifiers used in the report.
Quality Control 
Summary (Qc)
This section of the report includes the results of the laboratory quality control analyses required by procedure or 
analytical methods to assist in evaluating the validity of the results reported for your samples. These analyses are not 
being performed on your samples typically, but on laboratory generated material.
Sample Chain of 
Custody (Sc)
This is the document created in the field when your samples were initially collected. This is used to verify the time and 
date of collection, the person collecting the samples, and the analyses that the laboratory is requested to perform. This 
chain of custody also documents all persons (excluding commercial shippers) that have had control or possession of the 
samples from the time of collection until delivery to the laboratory for analysis.
Sample Results (Sr)
This section of your report will provide the results of all testing performed on your samples. These results are provided 
by sample ID and are separated by the analyses performed on each sample. The header line of each analysis section for
each sample will provide the name and method number for the analysis reported.
Sample Summary (Ss) This section of the Analytical Report defines the specific analyses performed for each sample ID, including the dates and
times of preparation and/or analysis.
Qualifier Description
B The same analyte is found in the associated blank.
J The identification of the analyte is acceptable; the reported value is an estimate.
J6 The sample matrix interfered with the ability to make any accurate determination; spike value is low.
V The sample concentration is too high to evaluate accurate spike recoveries.
